83	\end{figure}
84	
85	\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}
86	\label{sec:mirage}
87	
88	We hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. This \textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (\S\ref{sec:oracle}). We quantify mirage intensity via the \emph{Bias Ratio}:
89	\begin{equation}
90	\label{eq:mirage}
91	\text{BR}(M,T) = \frac{P(c \text{ supports } a_M \mid a_M \neq a^*)}{P(c \text{ supports } a^* \mid a_M \neq a^*)},
92	\end{equation}
93	where $c$ is a self-extracted constraint, $a_M$ the model's answer, and $a^*$ the ground truth. $\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints project model bias rather than provide independent verification. The mirage degrades aggregation through two channels: correlated constraints compress $K_{\text{eff}} < 2$ (\emph{direct}; Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}), and answer-coupled constraints collapse MAX-SAT to vote counting (\emph{indirect}; Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). Together with the oracle gap (\S\ref{sec:oracle}), BR bounds improvement: self-extracted constraints capture at most a $1/\text{BR}$ fraction of the oracle ceiling ($\Delta_{\max} \lesssim 6.0$\,pp for Mistral-7B, $\lesssim 3.6$\,pp for Qwen-14B; observed mean across conditions: $+2.12$\,pp). Three testable predictions follow: (1)~BR $> 1.0$ across architectures (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); (2)~no same-model intervention reduces BR below $1.0$ (\S\ref{sec:remediation}); (3)~only external verification escapes the mirage (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
94

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We identify the Symbolic Mirage: self-extracted symbolic constraints are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases (\S\ref{sec:mirage}). This mirage explains why constraint-based aggregation degenerates to vote counting (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), why process-level homogeneity exceeds answer-level agreement (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}), and why twenty-plus remediation strategies across fifteen families cannot break the same-model ceiling (\S\ref{sec:remediation}). Oracle controls confirm the mechanism is sound when given uncontaminated constraints (\S\ref{sec:oracle}); the mirage is the sole bottleneck. A cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that verification from outside the model's distribution escapes the mirage (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}). For practitioners, self-verification pipelines inheriting the model's own distribution cannot overcome confirmation bias; architecturally independent signals---whether from smaller specialized models, external tools, or human feedback---remain necessary for reliable constraint-based reasoning. Whether scale (70B+), trained extractors, or architectural diversity can shift the trade-off frontier remains open. Detecting and quantifying the Symbolic Mirage in other self-verification domains is a critical direction for trustworthy LLM reasoning.
4	
```

      "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe identify the Symbolic Mirage: self-extracted symbolic constraints are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). This mirage explains why constraint-based aggregation degenerates to vote counting (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), why process-level homogeneity exceeds answer-level agreement (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), and why twenty-plus remediation strategies across fifteen families cannot break the same-model ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). Oracle controls confirm the mechanism is sound when given uncontaminated constraints (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}); the mirage is the sole bottleneck. A cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that verification from outside the model's distribution escapes the mirage (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}). For practitioners, self-verification pipelines inheriting the model's own distribution cannot overcome confirmation bias; architecturally independent signals---whether from smaller specialized models, external tools, or human feedback---remain necessary for reliable constraint-based reasoning. Whether scale (70B+), trained extractors, or architectural diversity can shift the trade-off frontier remains open. Detecting and quantifying the Symbolic Mirage in other self-verification domains is a critical direction for trustworthy LLM reasoning.\n",
      "numLines": 4,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 4
    }
  },

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Mechanism and regime structure.}
5	The Symbolic Mirage (\S\ref{sec:mirage}) manifests through two degradation channels: the \emph{direct} channel limits voting diversity (Eff-$K < 2$), while the \emph{indirect} channel causes MAX-SAT to degenerate to vote counting (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). The bias ratio gradient ($4.97\times$ Mistral $> 3.93\times$ Qwen $> 2.88\times$ LLaMA) aligns with regime boundaries: high bias ($4.24\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias ($2.66\times$) at high SC yields the worst degradation ($-4.41$\,pp). Thinking-mode degeneration ($\kappa = 0.91$, Qwen3) confirms the trend continues with capability. For practitioners, $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy predicts when symbolic augmentation is unlikely to help.
6	
7	\paragraph{Broader implications.}
8	The independence-accuracy trade-off instantiates the classic ensemble tension between accuracy and diversity \citep{krogh1994neural, brown2005diversity}: the Symbolic Mirage maximizes individual accuracy but collapses voter disagreement, yielding $K_{\text{eff}} < 2$ despite nominal $K = 12$. CoT faithfulness compounds this: if traces do not reflect actual reasoning \citep{turpin2024language}, the mirage (Eq.~\ref{eq:mirage}) is an inherent property of extraction, not a correctable artifact.
9	
10	\paragraph{Relation to prior negative results.}
11	\citet{huang2024selfcorrect} and \citet{stechly2025selfverification} qualitatively conclude that LLMs cannot self-correct or self-verify without external feedback. Our work provides three quantitative extensions: (a)~$K_{\text{eff}}$ and BR are reusable continuous diagnostics applicable to any trace-aggregation system; (b)~formal Z3-checked FOL verification also fails, extending beyond prompt-level self-correction; and (c)~process-level cosine similarity ($0.69$--$0.79$ vs.\ $\kappa$ $0.41$--$0.74$) reveals a deeper homogeneity layer invisible to answer-only analysis. Three open conditions may shift the frontier: 70B+ models (though $\kappa = 0.91$ for thinking mode suggests bias may intensify), trained extractors decoupled from the reasoning distribution, and architecturally diverse verification (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
```

"After Benjamini--Hochberg correction for six comparisons ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ (adjusted $p < 0.001$); all four FOLIO comparisons are non-significant."

替换或紧跟在 "We report $w{=}3$ on ProofWriter as the primary finding" 之后。

## 2. TOST 等价性 → Abstract / §4 Experiments

**三分类框架（4/15 equivalence, 5/15 genuine difference, 6/15 underpowered）**是比 "fail" 或 "do not improve" 更精确的表述。

Abstract 中的关键句应改为类似：
"...none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$); TOST equivalence tests (margin $\pm 2$\,pp) establish equivalence for four conditions, while five show genuine SICA--SC differences---four of which are negative."

§4 主结果段也需要 1-2 句 TOST 总结。

## 3. α 敏感性 → Limitations 或 §3.2 footnote

"$\alpha$ sensitivity: accuracy varies by at most 0.49\,pp across $\alpha \in \{0.0, 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$ on Mistral-7B FOLIO, confirming that $\alpha = 0.5$ is not a critical hyperparameter."

一句话即可，放在 Limitations "Computational cost trade-off" 段或 §3.2 SICA Pipeline 末尾。

## 4. Constraint Rejection Rate → §5.2 或 §3.2

"Empirically, the MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\% of constraints on average ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| / |\mathcal{C}|$), with no significant difference between correct and incorrect predictions ($p = 0.636$), confirming that degeneration (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}) is the dominant regime."

放在 §5.2 confirmation bias 段或 §3.2 degeneration condition 段之后。

## 5. Pairwise Correlation → Limitations 或 §5.4 footnote

"Pairwise Pearson $r$ between traces (mean 0.625, Mistral-7B) is monotonically related to Fleiss' $\kappa$ across all four model families, supporting $\kappa$ as a valid consensus proxy despite the simplifying assumptions of Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}."

放在 Limitations "Statistical power" 段或 §5.4 末尾。

## 6. Lakens (2017) TOST 引用

在引用 TOST 的地方加 \citep{lakens2017equivalence}。BibTeX 条目由 Agent 写入。

_source line 12864 · 2026-05-20T15:46:29.011Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BC3qgV46h2EPaWNxVRHsMb`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}
2	\label{sec:solutions}
3	\label{sec:cross-arch}
4	
5	The analyses in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\S\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\S\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias and cannot break the same-model ceiling. The oracle gap (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the aggregation mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution---escaping the mirage entirely.
6	
7	\paragraph{Method.}
8	We test this with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \textsc{entailment}, \textsc{contradiction}, or \textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.
9	
10	\paragraph{Main result.}
11	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\%) and 55.00\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\% ($\Delta = +1.96$\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.125$, n.s.; $b{=}0$, $c{=}4$); $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\% ($+2.94$\,pp, $p = 0.210$, n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}1$ yields $+0.83$\,pp (n.s.), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\,pp ($p = 0.013$), and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\,pp ($p < 0.001$). Notably, same-model SICA \emph{degrades} ProofWriter performance ($-2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), while the cross-architecture combo yields significant improvement---illustrating the Symbolic Mirage prediction (\S\ref{sec:mirage}) that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution. We report $w{=}3$ on ProofWriter as the primary finding; higher weights risk overfitting. A per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\% of predictions) and performs poorly on \textsc{Unknown} (3.5\% accuracy), mirroring the FOLIO pattern where NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability. This approach operates outside the same-model scope of SICA (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Why it works.}
14	The 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, so they err on different subsets---satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\S\ref{sec:mirage}). On FOLIO, the gains show per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \textsc{False} (71.43\% vs.\ SC's 38.10\%) via contradiction detection, while SC dominates \textsc{Unknown} (68.12\% vs.\ 18.84\%); when both agree, accuracy reaches 68.48\% ($+14.07$\,pp).
15	
```

    "file": {
      "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/solutions.tex",
      "content": "\\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias and cannot break the same-model ceiling. The oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the aggregation mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution---escaping the mirage entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\\%) and 55.00\\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\\% ($\\Delta = +1.96$\\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.125$, n.s.; $b{=}0$, $c{=}4$); $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\\% ($+2.94$\\,pp, $p = 0.210$, n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}1$ yields $+0.83$\\,pp (n.s.), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\\,pp ($p = 0.013$), and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$). Notably, same-model SICA \\emph{degrades} ProofWriter performance ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), while the cross-architecture combo yields significant improvement---illustrating the Symbolic Mirage prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}) that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution. We report $w{=}3$ on ProofWriter as the primary finding; higher weights risk overfitting. A per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \\textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\\% of predictions) and performs poorly on \\textsc{Unknown} (3.5\\% accuracy), mirroring the FOLIO pattern where NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability. This approach operates outside the same-model scope of SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why it works.}\nThe 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, so they err on different subsets---satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). On FOLIO, the gains show per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \\textsc{False} (71.43\\% vs.\\ SC's 38.10\\%) via contradiction detection, while SC dominates \\textsc{Unknown} (68.12\\% vs.\\ 18.84\\%); when both agree, accuracy reaches 68.48\\% ($+14.07$\\,pp).\n",
      "numLines": 15,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 15
    }

Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples ($\Delta = +0.50$\,pp, n.s.) cannot break confirmation bias.
68	
69	\paragraph{Type C (SC $\leq$ 40\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\%$) shows $\Delta = -2.83$\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning.
70	
71	\paragraph{Degenerate cases.} When $C_0 < 5\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.
72

_source line 12867 · 2026-05-20T15:46:29.527Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019fZZnS92tsjhpha4RHi5jd`</summary>

```text
1	Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.
2	We investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.
3	Evaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.
4	We identify \emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\times$), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC.
5	Process-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.
6	Twenty-plus same-model remediation strategies spanning fifteen families all fail, revealing a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off at the 7B--32B scale: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\kappa \geq 0.40$ across three model families).
7	Oracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+29.90$\,pp and $+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO, $+11.50$\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$); same-model self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\% of the oracle ceiling.
8	However, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---shows consistent positive effects across two datasets ($+3.50$\,pp on ProofWriter, $p = 0.013$; McNemar exact test), suggesting that the oracle gap is partially recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.
9	
```

</details>

```text
1	\paragraph{Scale and language.}
2	All models range from 7B to 32B parameters; whether the independence bottleneck persists at larger scales (70B+) or with reasoning-specialized models is untested. All datasets are English-language logical reasoning benchmarks with discrete answer spaces. We do not evaluate trained FOL extractors, external solvers with verified grounding, or multilingual settings. The oracle analysis covers two formal logic benchmarks with gold annotations (FOLIO, ProofWriter); extending to commonsense or open-domain reasoning requires constructing comparable gold constraint sets.
3	
4	\paragraph{Statistical power.}
5	The per-label complementarity analysis of the cross-architecture NLI result (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}) involves small cell sizes (e.g., 42 \textsc{False} problems, 69 \textsc{Unknown} problems); per-label McNemar tests would be underpowered. The bias ratio metric ($4.97\times$) was initially computed on a single condition (Mistral-7B FOLIO, $n = 204$); cross-model replication (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) confirms the pattern but is limited to FOLIO. Four-seed replication shows 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results, and the intermediate-SC region is narrow (SC $\approx$ 50--55\%). The process-level similarity metric uses TF-IDF representations, which may underweight semantic similarity not captured by lexical overlap. Process-$\kappa$ provides aggregate-level characterization of reasoning homogeneity but does not discriminate per-instance (AUROC $\approx 0.5$).
6	
7	\paragraph{Computational cost trade-off.}
8	SICA requires $4.5\times$ the wall-clock time of SC (81.4 vs.\ 17.7\,min for 204 questions at $K{=}12$), with constraint extraction accounting for 76\% of overhead (\S\ref{sec:exp-setup}). This $3.5\times$ additional cost yields no significant accuracy gain on any condition, making the method impractical in its current form. Alternative prompting strategies (few-shot, chain-of-thought extraction) might improve extraction quality, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is structural. Additional limitations regarding power analysis and deduplication strategy are discussed in Appendix~\ref{app:limitations-extended}.
9	
10	\paragraph{Reproducibility.} All code, data, and experimental scripts will be released upon publication to facilitate replication of SICA, the oracle controls, and all remediation experiments.
11	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Utogv3J296NzYBM6N3Q74n`</summary>

```text
40	
41	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
42	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
43	
44	The trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.
45	
46	The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables; Z3 operates over logical structure via alpha-equivalence, mitigating this syntactic confound), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88\times$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The bias ratio gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\times$) at high SC produces the worst degradation ($-4.41$\,pp), because low confirmation bias at high SC means the model's consensus is already near-correct and constraints provide no leverage. The oracle ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\sim}$88\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.
47	
48	
49	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
50	\label{sec:process-kappa}
51	
52	Answer-level $\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:
53	\begin{equation}
54	\label{eq:process-sim}
55	\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \sum_{i < j} \cos(\mathbf{v}_i^q, \mathbf{v}_j^q),
56	\end{equation}
57	where $\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths. TF-IDF cosine similarity captures lexical overlap rather than semantic equivalence, making it a lower bound on true process similarity; semantic embedding methods would likely yield even higher values.
58	
59	\begin{figure}[t]
60	\centering
61	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}
62	\vspace{-2mm}
63	\caption{Answer agreement vs.\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}
64	\label{fig:process-kappa}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}
68	\label{sec:diagnostics}
69	
```

We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths. TF-IDF cosine similarity captures lexical overlap rather than semantic equivalence, making it a lower bound on true process similarity; semantic embedding methods would likely yield even higher values.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n",

    }

A per-label caveat:",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias and cannot break the same-model ceiling. The oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the aggregation mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution---escaping the mirage entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\\%) and 55.00\\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\\% ($\\Delta = +1.96$\\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.125$, n.s.; $b{=}0$, $c{=}4$); $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\\% ($+2.94$\\,pp, $p = 0.210$, n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}1$ yields $+0.83$\\,pp (n.s.), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\\,pp ($p = 0.013$), and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$). Notably, same-model SICA \\emph{degrades} ProofWriter performance ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), while the cross-architecture combo yields significant improvement---illustrating the Symbolic Mirage prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}) that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution. We report $w{=}3$ on ProofWriter as the primary finding; higher weights risk overfitting. A per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \\textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\\% of predictions) and performs poorly on \\textsc{Unknown} (3.5\\% accuracy), mirroring the FOLIO pattern where NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability. This approach operates outside the same-model scope of SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why it works.}\nThe 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, so they err on different subsets---satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). On FOLIO, the gains show per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \\textsc{False} (71.43\\% vs.\\ SC's 38.10\\%) via contradiction detection, while SC dominates \\textsc{Unknown} (68.12\\% vs.\\ 18.84\\%); when both agree, accuracy reaches 68.48\\% ($+14.07$\\,pp).\n",

    "newString": ", mirroring the FOLIO pattern where NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias and cannot break the same-model ceiling. The oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the aggregation mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution---escaping the mirage entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\\%) and 55.00\\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\\% ($\\Delta = +1.96$\\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.125$, n.s.; $b{=}0$, $c{=}4$); $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\\% ($+2.94$\\,pp, $p = 0.210$, n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}1$ yields $+0.83$\\,pp (n.s.), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\\,pp ($p = 0.013$), and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$). Notably, same-model SICA \\emph{degrades} ProofWriter performance ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), while the cross-architecture combo yields significant improvement---illustrating the Symbolic Mirage prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}) that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution. We report $w{=}3$ on ProofWriter as the primary finding; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction for six comparisons ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ (adjusted $p < 0.001$); all four FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. A per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \\textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\\% of predictions) and performs poorly on \\textsc{Unknown} (3.5\\% accuracy), mirroring the FOLIO pattern where NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability. This approach operates outside the same-model scope of SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why it works.}\nThe 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, so they err on different subsets---satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). On FOLIO, the gains show per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \\textsc{False} (71.43\\% vs.\\ SC's 38.10\\%) via contradiction detection, while SC dominates \\textsc{Unknown} (68.12\\% vs.\\ 18.84\\%); when both agree, accuracy reaches 68.48\\% ($+14.07$\\,pp).\n",

    "oldString": "we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.",
    "newString": "we find that self-extracted constraints do not improve majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect; TOST equivalence tests (margin $\\pm 2$\\,pp) establish equivalence for four conditions, while five show genuine SICA--SC differences---four of which are negative.",
    "originalFile": "Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.\nWe investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.\nEvaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.\nWe identify \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC.\nProcess-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.\nTwenty-plus same-model remediation strategies spanning fifteen families all fail, revealing a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off at the 7B--32B scale: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$ across three model families).\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+29.90$\\,pp and $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$); same-model self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\% of the oracle ceiling.\nHowever, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---shows consistent positive effects across two datasets ($+3.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter, $p = 0.013$; McNemar exact test), suggesting that the oracle gap is partially recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 6,
        "lines": [
          " Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.",
          " We investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.",
          "-Evaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.",
          "+Evaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints do not improve majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect; TOST equivalence tests (margin $\\pm 2$\\,pp) establish equivalence for four conditions, while five show genuine SICA--SC differences---four of which are negative.",
          " We identify \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC.",
          " Process-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.",
          " Twenty-plus same-model remediation strategies spanning fifteen families all fail, revealing a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off at the 7B--32B scale: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$ across three model families)."
        ]
      }
    ],
    "userModified": false,
    "replaceAll": false
  },

}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:exp-setup}\n\nWe evaluate SICA against Self-Consistency (SC) across six models on three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations across ${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances. Z3 timeout: 10s. $\\alpha{=}0.5$. $\\sim$50--55\\%) shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds: $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$) and $+2.94$\\,pp ($p = 0.070$); the remaining seeds show null or negative results. No seed reaches $p < 0.05$; the 4-seed mean is $+1.47$\\,pp. The default-seed and seed-456 runs share identical SC baselines (55.88\\%) yet diverge ($+3.43$ vs.\\ $0.00$\\,pp), suggesting that seed-level constraint quality, not SC level alone, influences the effect (entropy decomposition in Appendix~\\ref{app:entropy}). Mistral-7B on LogiQA, despite the same SC range, yields only $\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp ($p = 1.0$), attributable to domain formalizability (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}).\n\n\\paragraph{Type A (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%): confirmation bias.} All nine Type~A conditions (SC $\\geq 60\\%$, combined $N > 2{,}800$) produce no significant positive effects. The largest positive trend is LLaMA-3.1-8B on StrategyQA ($\\Delta = +2.00$\\,pp, $p = 0.424$, n.s.), while Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\\Delta = -2.50$\\,pp, $p = 0.011$). Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples ($\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp, n.s.) cannot break confirmation bias.\n\n\\paragraph{Type C (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\\%$) shows $\\Delta = -2.83$\\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \\emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{Degenerate cases.} When $C_0 < 5\\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.\n\nThe regime scatter (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime-scatter}, Appendix) confirms this pattern visually.\n\n\n\\subsection{Oracle Control}\n\\label{sec:oracle}\n\nOracle variants replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations: FOL from FOLIO ($n = 204$) and DSL from ProofWriter ($n = 600$), where depth-stratified rules are programmatically derived, requiring no human annotation. Same-model Oracle-SICA on Mistral-7B achieves 85.78\\% on FOLIO ($\\Delta = +29.90$\\,pp, $p < 10^{-13}$); Qwen2.5-14B achieves 89.71\\% ($\\Delta = +14.22$\\,pp, $p = 2.0 \\times 10^{-7}$) and 86.67\\% on ProofWriter ($\\Delta = +11.50$\\,pp, $p = 9.21 \\times 10^{-15}$). Z3 agrees with gold labels on 95.1\\% (FOLIO) and 97.1\\% (ProofWriter) of problems. The larger ceiling for weaker models ($+29.90$ vs.\\ $+14.22$\\,pp) is consistent with the bias ratio gradient (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\n\nThese results establish that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given discriminative constraints. Mistral-7B's best self-extracted result ($+3.43$\\,pp) captures only 11.5\\% of its same-model oracle ceiling ($+29.90$\\,pp). The remaining ${\\sim}$88\\% is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n",

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\\{q : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}| / |\\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\\{q : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}| / |\\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC. The penalty weight $\\alpha = 0.5$ is not a critical hyperparameter: accuracy varies by at most $0.49$\\,pp across $\\alpha \\in \\{0.0, 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$ on Mistral-7B FOLIO.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Background \\& Setup}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}\n\\label{sec:condorcet}\n\nSelf-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees that if $K$ independent voters are each correct with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth as $K \\to \\infty$ \\citep{austensmith1996}. LLM traces violate this independence: all $K$ samples come from the same model with only temperature as variation. Following \\citet{ladha1992correlated} and the design-effect framework of \\citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:effk}\nK_{\\text{eff}} = \\frac{K}{1 + (K - 1) \\cdot \\kappa}.\n\\end{equation}\nIn our experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across instruction-tuned pairs (0.91 for thinking mode), yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} \\approx 1.2\\text{--}3.6$ for $K = 12$. Note that $\\kappa$ is a qualitative proxy for the intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC); we use $K_{\\text{eff}}$ as an interpretive indicator rather than a precise statistical estimator. This \\emph{independence bottleneck} motivates our investigation.\n\n\\subsection{SICA Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:pipeline}\n\nSymbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\\{t_1, \\ldots, t_K\\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts FOL constraints from each trace at $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}). The \\emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:maxsat}\n\\mathcal{C}^* = \\arg\\max_{\\mathcal{S} \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}} \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{S}} w_c \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\mathcal{S} \\text{ is satisfiable.}\n\\end{equation}\nZ3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\\texttt{answer\\_a} == \\texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.\n\n\\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus} = \\mathcal{C} \\setminus \\mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:score}\n\\text{score}(a) = \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a) - \\alpha \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a} \\text{score}(a)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Degeneration condition.} If self-extracted constraints are coupled to their source answer, MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nequivalent to majority voting. We show in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias} that this degeneration occurs in practice.\n\n\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\\{q : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}| / |\\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n    node distance=0.4cm and 0.35cm,\n    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.6cm,\n                text width=1.6cm, align=center, font=\\scriptsize},\n    arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick},\n    darr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick, densely dashed},\n    lbl/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!70!black},\n    ibottle/.style={draw=red!70!black, densely dashed, rounded corners=3pt,\n                    inner sep=3pt},\n]\n\\node[box] (q) {Question $q$};\n\\node[box, right=of q] (sample) {Sample $K$\\\\traces};\n\\node[box, right=of sample] (extract) {Extract FOL\\\\constraints};\n\\node[box, right=of extract] (dedup) {Dedup.\\ +\\\\weight};\n\\node[box, right=of dedup] (maxsat) {MAX-SAT\\\\(Z3)};\n\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of maxsat, fill=gray!12] (ans) {Answer $a^*$};\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of sample, fill=gray!12] (sc) {Majority\\\\vote};\n\n\\draw[arr] (q) -- (sample);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- (extract);\n\\draw[arr] (extract) -- (dedup);\n\\draw[arr] (dedup) -- (maxsat);\n\\draw[arr] (maxsat) -- (ans);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- node[lbl, right, yshift=0pt] {SC} (sc);\n\n\\node[lbl, above=0.01cm of maxsat] {SICA};\n\n\\node[ibottle, fit=(sample)(extract), label={[font=\\scriptsize\\itshape, text=red!70!black]above:Independence Bottleneck}] {};\n\n\\draw[darr, gray!60] ([xshift=-0.1cm]dedup.south) -- ++(0,-0.35) node[lbl, below, yshift=-0.05cm] {oracle: gold FOL};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{The SICA pipeline. SC selects the majority answer from $K$ traces. SICA extracts FOL constraints, deduplicates, and solves MAX-SAT via Z3. Red dashed box: the independence bottleneck. Dashed arrow: oracle variant with gold FOL.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}\n\\label{sec:mirage}\n\nWe hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. This \\textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}). We quantify mirage intensity via the \\emph{Bias Ratio}:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mirage}\n\\text{BR}(M,T) = \\frac{P(c \\text{ supports } a_M \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)}{P(c \\text{ supports } a^* \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $c$ is a self-extracted constraint, $a_M$ the model's answer, and $a^*$ the ground truth. $\\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints project model bias rather than provide independent verification. The mirage degrades aggregation through two channels: correlated constraints compress $K_{\\text{eff}} < 2$ (\\emph{direct}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}), and answer-coupled constraints collapse MAX-SAT to vote counting (\\emph{indirect}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Together with the oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}), BR bounds improvement: self-extracted constraints capture at most a $1/\\text{BR}$ fraction of the oracle ceiling ($\\Delta_{\\max} \\lesssim 6.0$\\,pp for Mistral-7B, $\\lesssim 3.6$\\,pp for Qwen-14B; observed mean across conditions: $+2.12$\\,pp). Three testable predictions follow: (1)~BR $> 1.0$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); (2)~no same-model intervention reduces BR below $1.0$ (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}); (3)~only external verification escapes the mirage (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section tests the three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nThe trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \\emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables; Z3 operates over logical structure via alpha-equivalence, mitigating this syntactic confound), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The bias ratio gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\\times$) at high SC produces the worst degradation ($-4.41$\\,pp), because low confirmation bias at high SC means the model's consensus is already near-correct and constraints provide no leverage. The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\\sim}$88\\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths. TF-IDF cosine similarity captures lexical overlap rather than semantic equivalence, making it a lower bound on true process similarity; semantic embedding methods would likely yield even higher values.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Ten further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.",

The bias ratio gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\\times$) at high SC produces the worst degradation ($-4.41$\\,pp), because low confirmation bias at high SC means the model's consensus is already near-correct and constraints provide no leverage. The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\\sim}$88\\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths. TF-IDF cosine similarity captures lexical overlap rather than semantic equivalence, making it a lower bound on true process similarity; semantic embedding methods would likely yield even higher values.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Ten further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.",

    "originalFile": "\\paragraph{Scale and language.}\nAll models range from 7B to 32B parameters; whether the independence bottleneck persists at larger scales (70B+) or with reasoning-specialized models is untested. All datasets are English-language logical reasoning benchmarks with discrete answer spaces. We do not evaluate trained FOL extractors, external solvers with verified grounding, or multilingual settings. The oracle analysis covers two formal logic benchmarks with gold annotations (FOLIO, ProofWriter); extending to commonsense or open-domain reasoning requires constructing comparable gold constraint sets.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical power.}\nThe per-label complementarity analysis of the cross-architecture NLI result (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) involves small cell sizes (e.g., 42 \\textsc{False} problems, 69 \\textsc{Unknown} problems); per-label McNemar tests would be underpowered. The bias ratio metric ($4.97\\times$) was initially computed on a single condition (Mistral-7B FOLIO, $n = 204$); cross-model replication (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) confirms the pattern but is limited to FOLIO. Four-seed replication shows 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results, and the intermediate-SC region is narrow (SC $\\approx$ 50--55\\%). The process-level similarity metric uses TF-IDF representations, which may underweight semantic similarity not captured by lexical overlap. Process-$\\kappa$ provides aggregate-level characterization of reasoning homogeneity but does not discriminate per-instance (AUROC $\\approx 0.5$).\n\n\\paragraph{Computational cost trade-off.}\nSICA requires $4.5\\times$ the wall-clock time of SC (81.4 vs.\\ 17.7\\,min for 204 questions at $K{=}12$), with constraint extraction accounting for 76\\% of overhead (\\S\\ref{sec:exp-setup}). This $3.5\\times$ additional cost yields no significant accuracy gain on any condition, making the method impractical in its current form. Alternative prompting strategies (few-shot, chain-of-thought extraction) might improve extraction quality, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is structural. Additional limitations regarding power analysis and deduplication strategy are discussed in Appendix~\\ref{app:limitations-extended}.\n\n\\paragraph{Reproducibility.} All code, data, and experimental scripts will be released upon publication to facilitate replication of SICA, the oracle controls, and all remediation experiments.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\paragraph{Scale and language.}\nAll models range from 7B to 32B parameters; whether the independence bottleneck persists at larger scales (70B+) or with reasoning-specialized models is untested. All datasets are English-language logical reasoning benchmarks with discrete answer spaces. We do not evaluate trained FOL extractors, external solvers with verified grounding, or multilingual settings. The oracle analysis covers two formal logic benchmarks with gold annotations (FOLIO, ProofWriter); extending to commonsense or open-domain reasoning requires constructing comparable gold constraint sets.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical power.}\nThe per-label complementarity analysis of the cross-architecture NLI result (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) involves small cell sizes (e.g., 42 \\textsc{False} problems, 69 \\textsc{Unknown} problems); per-label McNemar tests would be underpowered. The bias ratio metric ($4.97\\times$) was initially computed on a single condition (Mistral-7B FOLIO, $n = 204$); cross-model replication (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) confirms the pattern but is limited to FOLIO. Four-seed replication shows 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results, and the intermediate-SC region is narrow (SC $\\approx$ 50--55\\%). The process-level similarity metric uses TF-IDF representations (a lower bound on true process similarity). Pairwise Pearson $r$ between traces (mean $0.625$, Mistral-7B) is monotonically related to Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across all four model families, supporting $\\kappa$ as a valid consensus proxy despite the simplifying assumptions of Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational cost trade-off.}\nSICA requires $4.5\\times$ the wall-clock time of SC (81.4 vs.\\ 17.7\\,min for 204 questions at $K{=}12$), with constraint extraction accounting for 76\\% of overhead (\\S\\ref{sec:exp-setup}). This $3.5\\times$ additional cost yields no significant accuracy gain on any condition, making the method impractical in its current form. Alternative prompting strategies (few-shot, chain-of-thought extraction) might improve extraction quality, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is structural. Additional limitations regarding power analysis and deduplication strategy are discussed in Appendix~\\ref{app:limitations-extended}.\n\n\\paragraph{Reproducibility.} All code, data, and experimental scripts will be released upon publication to facilitate replication of SICA, the oracle controls, and all remediation experiments.\n",

673	
674	\section{Extended Limitations}
675	\label{app:limitations-extended}
676	
677	Beyond the limitations discussed in the main text, several additional constraints bound our conclusions. Seven of our eleven model-domain conditions have $n \leq 60$, providing insufficient statistical power for confirmatory claims; power analysis shows that the minimum detectable effect exceeds 5\,pp for these conditions (Table~\ref{tab:power}), so they serve as regime characterization rather than definitive tests. Our extraction prompts use a single zero-shot template per domain; alternative prompting strategies (few-shot exemplars, chain-of-thought extraction, or iterative refinement) might yield higher-quality constraints, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is structural rather than prompt-specific. The constraint quality analysis (91.8\% discriminative rate, 39.2\% source-trace minority weight) covers Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO + ProofWriter ($N = 804$); other model-domain combinations may exhibit different characteristics. Constraint extraction uses $T_{\text{ext}} = 0.3$ while trace sampling uses $T = 0.7$; alternative extraction temperatures might yield different results. Although we prompt for FOL-style formalizations, extracted constraints are predominantly propositional ground clauses without quantifiers (\S\ref{sec:constraint-extraction}); whether models capable of genuine quantified FOL would yield higher discriminative rates is untested. Deduplication uses syntactic equivalence, so semantically equivalent constraints with different surface forms receive separate weights. The GSM8K pilot ($n = 30$) illustrates the constraint-absent regime and should not be interpreted as confirmatory evidence.
678	
```

  "new_string": "\\section{TOST Equivalence Tests}\n\\label{app:tost}\n\nWe apply two one-sided tests (TOST) \\citep{lakens2017equivalence} with equivalence margin $\\pm 2$\\,pp to all 15 non-degenerate SICA--SC comparisons. Four conditions fall within the equivalence bounds (Qwen2.5-7B FOLIO, Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO, Mistral-7B LogiQA, Qwen3-14B PW-D5), confirming that SICA and SC produce statistically equivalent accuracy. Five conditions show genuine differences outside the equivalence margin: four are negative (PW-D5 Mistral $-2.83$\\,pp, PW-D5 Qwen-14B $-2.50$\\,pp, FOLIO LLaMA $-4.41$\\,pp, FOLIO Qwen3 $-0.49$\\,pp with tight CI) and one is positive but non-significant (Mistral FOLIO $+3.43$\\,pp, $p = 0.092$). The remaining six conditions are underpowered: the equivalence test is inconclusive and the superiority test is also non-significant, leaving these conditions in an indeterminate zone.\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations-extended}"
}

**Error Taxonomy (exp-033 Mistral-7B FOLIO-204, both_wrong=81 题)**:
- **64% (52/81)** 的 both_wrong 是 gt=False/True 但 SICA+SC 都预测 Unknown → 系统性"拿不准就说 Unknown"回避策略
- gt=False→Unknown: 32 题（最大单一错误模式）
- gt=True→Unknown: 20 题
- **confident_systematic_error**: 24 题 — 12 条 trace 高度一致 + SICA/SC 同意，但全错 → confirmation bias 教科书案例
- **borderline_sica**: 6 题 — SICA margin 极小(<10)，走向错误一边

**Gold Label Skew**:
- both_wrong 中 False 占 43% vs both_correct 中仅 21% → LLM 对否定推理最弱
- both_wrong 中 Unknown 仅 23% vs both_correct 中 42%

**Constraint 密度悖论**:
- both_wrong 平均 41.9 constraints vs both_correct 37.1 → more constraints ≠ better，支持"bias amplification"论点

1. confident_systematic_error (24 cases) 是 Symbolic Confirmation Bias (Claim 5) 的直接案例证据
2. Gold label skew (False hardest) 可解释为什么 NLI verifier (DeBERTa) 在 contradiction detection 上互补 — LLM 弱在 False，NLI 强在 contradiction

```text
40	
41	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
42	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
43	
44	The trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\% of constraints on average ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| / |\mathcal{C}|$), with no significant difference between correct and incorrect predictions ($p = 0.636$), confirming that degeneration (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}) is the dominant regime.
45	
46	The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables; Z3 operates over logical structure via alpha-equivalence, mitigating this syntactic confound), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88\times$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The bias ratio gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\times$) at high SC produces the worst degradation ($-4.41$\,pp), because low confirmation bias at high SC means the model's consensus is already near-correct and constraints provide no leverage. The oracle ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\sim}$88\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.
47	
48	
49	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012JTTBJZMxxPsbScj4qd1Yq`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}
2	\label{sec:solutions}
3	\label{sec:cross-arch}
4	
5	The analyses in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\S\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\S\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias and cannot break the same-model ceiling. The oracle gap (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the aggregation mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution---escaping the mirage entirely.
6	
7	\paragraph{Method.}
8	We test this with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \textsc{entailment}, \textsc{contradiction}, or \textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.
9	
10	\paragraph{Main result.}
11	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\%) and 55.00\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\% ($\Delta = +1.96$\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.125$, n.s.; $b{=}0$, $c{=}4$); $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\% ($+2.94$\,pp, $p = 0.210$, n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}1$ yields $+0.83$\,pp (n.s.), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\,pp ($p = 0.013$), and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\,pp ($p < 0.001$). Notably, same-model SICA \emph{degrades} ProofWriter performance ($-2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), while the cross-architecture combo yields significant improvement---illustrating the Symbolic Mirage prediction (\S\ref{sec:mirage}) that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution. We report $w{=}3$ on ProofWriter as the primary finding; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction for six comparisons ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ (adjusted $p < 0.001$); all four FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. A per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\% of predictions) and performs poorly on \textsc{Unknown} (3.5\% accuracy), mirroring the FOLIO pattern where NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.
12	
13	\paragraph{Why it works.}
14	The 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, so they err on different subsets---satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\S\ref{sec:mirage}). On FOLIO, the gains show per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \textsc{False} (71.43\% vs.\ SC's 38.10\%) via contradiction detection, while SC dominates \textsc{Unknown} (68.12\% vs.\ 18.84\%); when both agree, accuracy reaches 68.48\% ($+14.07$\,pp).
15	
```

On FOLIO, the gains show per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \\textsc{False} (71.43\\% vs.\\ SC's 38.10\\%) via contradiction detection, while SC dominates \\textsc{Unknown} (68.12\\% vs.\\ 18.84\\%); when both agree, accuracy reaches 68.48\\% ($+14.07$\\,pp).\n",
