        "content": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section tests the three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nThe trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \\emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\\% of constraints on average ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| / |\\mathcal{C}|$, $p = 0.636$ between correct/incorrect), confirming degeneration (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Counterintuitively, jointly-wrong problems produce \\emph{more} constraints (41.9 vs.\\ 37.1 for correct): higher symbolic density amplifies rather than corrects bias.\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% zero cross-answer variable overlap), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). A premise-only experiment isolates the bias source: filtering out the 23\\% of constraints that reference the conclusion halves the accuracy gain ($+2.45$\\,pp, $p = 0.359$, n.s.), indicating that conclusion-referencing constraints account for approximately half of the constraint error. Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The bias ratio gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\\times$) at high SC produces the worst degradation ($-4.41$\\,pp). Error analysis reveals 64\\% (52/81) of joint SICA+SC errors predict \\textsc{Unknown} for ground-truth \\textsc{True}/\\textsc{False} problems---a systematic ``hedge to Unknown'' avoidance strategy---and 30\\% (24/81) are \\emph{confident systematic errors} where all 12 traces agree on the wrong answer. \\textsc{False} problems are disproportionately hard: 43\\% of joint errors vs.\\ 21\\% of correct predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-categorization}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\\sim}$88\\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Thirteen further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, premise-only filtering, DPP trace selection, and alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.",
        "numLines": 85,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 85
      }
    },

  },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/solutions.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias and cannot break the same-model ceiling. The oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the aggregation mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution---escaping the mirage entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\\%) and 55.00\\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\\% ($\\Delta = +1.96$\\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.125$, n.s.; $b{=}0$, $c{=}4$); $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\\% ($+2.94$\\,pp, $p = 0.210$, n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}1$ yields $+0.83$\\,pp (n.s.), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\\,pp ($p = 0.013$), and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$). Notably, same-model SICA \\emph{degrades} ProofWriter performance ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), while the cross-architecture combo yields significant improvement---illustrating the Symbolic Mirage prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}) that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution. We report $w{=}3$ on ProofWriter as the primary finding; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction for six comparisons ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ (adjusted $p < 0.001$); all four FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. A per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \\textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\\% of predictions) and performs poorly on \\textsc{Unknown} (3.5\\% accuracy), mirroring the FOLIO pattern where NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.\n\n\\paragraph{Why it works.}\nThe 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, so they err on different subsets---satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). On FOLIO, this produces per-label complementarity precisely where the LLM is weakest: \\textsc{False} problems constitute 43\\% of joint errors (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), and NLI excels exactly there (71.43\\% vs.\\ SC's 38.10\\%) via contradiction detection, while SC dominates \\textsc{Unknown} (68.12\\% vs.\\ 18.84\\%).\n",

        "totalLines": 15
      }

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\paragraph{Self-Consistency and extensions.}\nSC selects the most frequent answer across chain-of-thought traces \\citep{wang2023sc}. Extensions include universal SC for free-form generation \\citep{chen2024usc}, confidence weighting \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, diverse prompting with step-aware verification \\citep{li2023diverse}, program-based vote filtering \\citep{toh2024notallvotes}, and reasoning-aware filtering \\citep{wan2024rasc, kim2026reasc}. \\citet{loo2025reevaluating} show that SC gains plateau early. Our Condorcet analysis explains this: $\\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ compresses $K_{\\text{eff}}$ to 1.20--3.62 for $K = 12$ (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}).\n\n\\paragraph{Neuro-symbolic reasoning.}\nLogic-LM \\citep{pan2023logiclm} and LINC \\citep{olausson2023linc} translate problems into FOL for external provers; LINC documents ${\\sim}$40\\% translation errors. Recent extensions---CLOVER \\citep{ryu2025clover}, DyLoS \\citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \\citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \\citep{wang2025fover}, VERGE \\citep{singh2026verge}, LogicAgent \\citep{luo2025logicagent}---add compositional translation, solver selection, decomposition, trained verifiers, and formal refinement, each introducing capabilities beyond the reasoning model. \\citet{amonkar2025realitycheck} show that LLM formalization degrades with complexity; \\citet{chan2026position} argue that logical soundness is not a reliable criterion for neurosymbolic verification with LLMs. Our oracle controls confirm the mechanism is sound ($+29.90$/$+14.22$\\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter); the failure localizes to self-extraction quality (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\n\n\\paragraph{Test-time compute and verification.}\nScaling test-time computation through additional traces \\citep{snell2025scaling} faces the same correlation challenge: same-model attempts collapse into correlated solutions. Reasoning models (o1, DeepSeek-R1 \\citep{guo2025deepseekr1}, QwQ \\citep{qwen2024qwq}) extend thinking time but remain subject to the independence bottleneck: QwQ-32B produces $\\kappa = 0.91$ and $C_0 = 2.6\\%$, suggesting that longer reasoning chains intensify homogenization rather than alleviating it. Trained verifiers offer an alternative \\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k, lightman2024prm, wang2024mathshepherd}; multi-agent debate refines answers through discussion \\citep{liang2023encouraging, du2023improving}. Self-correction without external feedback degrades performance \\citep{huang2024selfcorrect, stechly2025selfverification}, paralleling our confirmation bias finding (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\n\n\\paragraph{CJT and ensemble diversity.}\nCJT \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees convergence under independent voting; \\citet{ladha1992correlated} prove correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size; \\citet{lefort2024condorcet_llm} apply CJT to LLM classification. We extend this by quantifying \\emph{why} self-correction fails---same-model traces are correlated voters ($K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapses)---and showing this persists even under formal verification (Z3-checked FOL) and at the process level (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).",
        "numLines": 14,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 14
      }
    },

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	This section tests the three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\S\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\text{eff}}$ collapse (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\S\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\S\ref{sec:remediation}).
5	
6	\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}
7	\label{sec:effective-k}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.20\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\kappa \in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\text{eff}}$ ($\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\kappa \geq 0.41$ (Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\small
14	\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\kappa$ descending). $\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$, 4 traces each.}
15	\label{tab:independence}
16	\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}
17	\toprule
18	Model & Condition & $\kappa$ & $K_{\text{eff}}$ & SC (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
19	\midrule
20	Qwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\sim}$84 & ${\sim}$0 \\
21	Qwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\
22	Qwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\
23	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\
24	Qwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\
25	\addlinespace
26	Mistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\
27	Mistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\
28	Mistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\
29	Mistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\
30	Mistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\
31	Mistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\
32	Mistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\
33	Mistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\
34	Mistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\
35	Mistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\
36	\bottomrule
37	\end{tabular}
38	\end{table}
39	
40	
41	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
42	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
43	
44	The trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\% of constraints on average ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| / |\mathcal{C}|$, $p = 0.636$ between correct/incorrect), confirming degeneration (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). Counterintuitively, jointly-wrong problems produce \emph{more} constraints (41.9 vs.\ 37.1 for correct): higher symbolic density amplifies rather than corrects bias.
45	
46	The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints use trace-specific variable names (56.4\% zero cross-answer variable overlap), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). A premise-only experiment isolates the bias source: filtering out the 23\% of constraints that reference the conclusion halves the accuracy gain ($+2.45$\,pp, $p = 0.359$, n.s.), indicating that conclusion-referencing constraints account for approximately half of the constraint error. Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88\times$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The bias ratio gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\times$) at high SC produces the worst degradation ($-4.41$\,pp). Error analysis reveals 64\% (52/81) of joint SICA+SC errors predict \textsc{Unknown} for ground-truth \textsc{True}/\textsc{False} problems---a systematic ``hedge to Unknown'' avoidance strategy---and 30\% (24/81) are \emph{confident systematic errors} where all 12 traces agree on the wrong answer. \textsc{False} problems are disproportionately hard: 43\% of joint errors vs.\ 21\% of correct predictions (Appendix~\ref{app:error-categorization}). The oracle ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\sim}$88\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.
47	
48	
49	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
50	\label{sec:process-kappa}
51	
52	Answer-level $\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:
53	\begin{equation}
54	\label{eq:process-sim}
55	\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \sum_{i < j} \cos(\mathbf{v}_i^q, \mathbf{v}_j^q),
56	\end{equation}
57	where $\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.
58	
59	\begin{figure}[t]
60	\centering
61	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}
62	\vspace{-2mm}
63	\caption{Answer agreement vs.\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}
64	\label{fig:process-kappa}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}
68	\label{sec:diagnostics}
69	
70	Further diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}).
71	
72	
73	\subsection{Remediation Analysis}
74	\label{sec:remediation}
75	\label{sec:interventions}
76	
77	We test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.
78	
79	\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\Delta = -1.1$\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\pm 1$\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$) yields $\Delta = 0.00$\,pp.
80	
81	\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\Delta \approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\Delta = -9.31$\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\ref{app:remediation}).
82	
83	\paragraph{Additional directions.} Thirteen further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, premise-only filtering, DPP trace selection, and alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).
84	
85	\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp. All families cluster above $\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.
```

Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Thirteen further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, premise-only filtering, DPP trace selection, and alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.",

```text
1	\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}
2	\label{sec:solutions}
3	\label{sec:cross-arch}
4	
5	The analyses in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\S\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\S\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias and cannot break the same-model ceiling. The oracle gap (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the aggregation mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution---escaping the mirage entirely.
6	
7	\paragraph{Method.}
8	We test this with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \textsc{entailment}, \textsc{contradiction}, or \textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.
9	
10	\paragraph{Main result.}
11	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\%) and 55.00\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\% ($\Delta = +1.96$\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.125$, n.s.; $b{=}0$, $c{=}4$); $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\% ($+2.94$\,pp, $p = 0.210$, n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}1$ yields $+0.83$\,pp (n.s.), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\,pp ($p = 0.013$), and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\,pp ($p < 0.001$). Notably, same-model SICA \emph{degrades} ProofWriter performance ($-2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), while the cross-architecture combo yields significant improvement---illustrating the Symbolic Mirage prediction (\S\ref{sec:mirage}) that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution. We report $w{=}3$ on ProofWriter as the primary finding; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction for six comparisons ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ (adjusted $p < 0.001$); all four FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. A per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\% of predictions) and performs poorly on \textsc{Unknown} (3.5\% accuracy), mirroring the FOLIO pattern where NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.
12	
13	\paragraph{Why it works.}
14	The 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, so they err on different subsets---satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\S\ref{sec:mirage}). On FOLIO, this produces per-label complementarity precisely where the LLM is weakest: \textsc{False} problems constitute 43\% of joint errors (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), and NLI excels exactly there (71.43\% vs.\ SC's 38.10\%) via contradiction detection, while SC dominates \textsc{Unknown} (68.12\% vs.\ 18.84\%).
15	
```

11	\section{Prompt Templates}
12	\label{app:prompts}
13	
14	SICA uses domain-specific prompt templates for constraint extraction: one for mathematical reasoning (GSM8K, AIME) and one for logical reasoning (ProofWriter, FOLIO). Both share the same system prompt. The LLM is called with temperature $T{=}0.3$, max tokens $1024$, and structured JSON output mode. Up to 6 traces are processed in parallel via thread pooling, with 2 retries on failure (exponential backoff, base 0.5s).
15	
16	\paragraph{System Prompt.}
17	
18	\begin{lstlisting}
19	You are a constraint extraction tool. Output ONLY
20	valid JSON. Do NOT solve problems, reason about
21	correctness, or think step-by-step. Extract
22	constraints from the given trace exactly as stated.
23	\end{lstlisting}
24	
25	\paragraph{Mathematical Constraint Extraction (EXTRACTION\_PROMPT).}
26	
27	Used for GSM8K and AIME datasets. The placeholder \texttt{\{trace\}} is replaced with the reasoning trace at runtime.
28	
29	\begin{lstlisting}
30	Extract mathematical constraints from this reasoning
31	trace as a JSON object.
32	
33	Rules:
34	- type: "equation", "inequality", or "predicate"
35	- expression: human-readable, e.g. "x + y == 14"
36	- z3_formula: a single comparison expression in
37	  Python Z3 syntax
38	- source_step: step number where the constraint
39	  appears
40	- Use variable names from the trace
41	
42	z3_formula MUST follow these rules:
43	- Allowed operators: ==, !=, <, >, <=, >=, +, -, *,
44	  /, **, %
45	- Use individual named variables: a1, a2, a3...
46	  NOT arrays a[1] or sets A
47	- Expand all sums explicitly:
48	  "a1 + a2 + a3 == 55" NOT "sum([a1,a2,a3])"
49	- NO Python builtins: sum(), len(), max(), min(),
50	  list(), set(), range(), int()
51	- Each z3_formula must be exactly ONE comparison
52	  (==, !=, <, >, <=, >=)
53	- For multiple values, write separate constraints
54	  for each
55	
56	Output ONLY the JSON below, nothing else:
57	{"constraints": [{"type": "equation",
58	  "expression": "x + y == 14",
59	  "z3_formula": "x + y == 14",
60	  "source_step": 1}],
61	 "answer": "final_answer",
62	 "variables": ["x", "y"]}
63	
64	Trace:
65	{trace}
66	\end{lstlisting}
67	
68	\paragraph{Logical Constraint Extraction (LOGIC\_EXTRACTION\_PROMPT).}
69	
70	Used for ProofWriter and FOLIO datasets.
71	
72	\begin{lstlisting}
73	IMPORTANT: You are an EXTRACTION tool, NOT a problem
74	solver.
75	- Do NOT re-solve or re-reason about the problem.
76	- Do NOT verify whether the trace's reasoning is
77	  correct.
78	- ONLY extract the logical constraints that are
79	  explicitly stated or used in the trace below.
80	- Output ONLY the JSON object, nothing else.
81	
82	Extract logical constraints from this reasoning trace
83	about a logic problem.
84	
85	The problem has facts (base assertions), rules
86	(grounded implications), and derived conclusions.
87	Convert each into a Z3 boolean formula.
88	
89	Variable naming:

94	- Use lowercase with underscores only
95

98	  e.g., kind_anne == True

434	15 & DeepConf (logprob) & Filtering & Confidence & 0.0 & $\Delta_{\text{logprob}} = 0.006$ (no separation) \\
435	16 & Cross-model SICA & Cross-model & Independence & $-$0.49 & $\kappa_{\text{cross}}{=}0.42$ vs $\kappa_{\text{within}}{=}0.53$ \\
436	17 & Problem perturbation & Input diversity & Trace diversity & $<$0 & $\kappa$ \emph{increased} to 0.70 (anti-effect) \\
437	18 & MIN-UNSAT & Solver objective & Constraint sel.\ & $-$6.5 & Mean excluded constraints $= 1.3$ \\
438	19 & $\kappa$-aware SC & Voting rule & Weighted voting & 0.0 & 11/11 conditions $\Delta = 0$ \\
439	20 & Z3 trace filter & Trace filtering & Quality & $-$0.49 & Precision 49.1\%, 7 questions affected \\
440	21 & Bootstrapped gold & Iterative & Self-improvement & 0.0$^\dagger$ & R1 $+$2.45\,pp then oscillates \\
441	22 & Selective abstention & Diagnostic & Confidence & $+$4.4$^*$ & AUROC $= 0.673$, at 80\% coverage \\
442	23 & ASCII perturbation & Input diversity & Format diversity & 0.0 & $\kappa$: $0.39 \to 0.36$ (negligible) \\
443	\addlinespace
444	24 & Atom pipeline & Paradigm & Decomposition & $-$10.0 & Atomic extraction degrades \\
445	25 & Premise-only filter & Constraint sel.\ & Bias isolation & $+$2.45 & $p = 0.359$; halves conclusion-bias \\
446	26 & DPP trace selection & Trace diversity & Determinantal & $+$1.47 & $p = 0.629$; $K_{\text{sel}}{=}8$ \\
447	\bottomrule
448	\end{tabular}\\[2pt]
449	{\footnotesize All experiments on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n{=}204$) unless noted. $^\dagger$\,Oscillates without convergence; net effect $\approx 0$. $^*$\,Accuracy gain only at reduced coverage (diagnostic value, not a general improvement). Methods 1--12 are reported in Table~\ref{tab:remediation} and Table~\ref{tab:method_interventions}.}

455	\caption{Summary of 22+ intervention strategies grouped by category. All produce neutral-to-negative accuracy changes relative to Self-Consistency. The LLM-Debate bar is truncated at $-11.5$\,pp (actual: $-18.6$\,pp). The grey band indicates $\pm 1$\,pp noise zone.}
456	\label{fig:interventions-summary}

458	
459	
460	\section{Extraction Error Taxonomy}
461	\label{app:extraction}
462	\label{sec:extraction-taxonomy}
463	
464	Constraints must discriminate between candidate answers for MAX-SAT to produce a ranking different from majority vote. We measure discriminative power at two levels on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). At the source-trace level, 39.2\% of total constraint weight originates from traces whose answer differs from the plurality, but only 12.1\% of individual constraints fall into this minority category. At the Z3 semantic level, 91.8\% of constraints [95\% CI: 91.2, 92.4] are technically discriminative, producing different satisfiability outcomes when evaluated against different candidate answers. Only 8.2\% are non-discriminative.
465	
466	The high discriminative rate does not translate into accuracy gains because discrimination mirrors the model's own answer distribution. Each trace produces constraints with its own variable names, so Z3 treats identically-intended propositions as unrelated symbols; among cross-answer constraint pairs, 56.4\% share zero Z3 variables. This variable isolation means constraints from different traces are trivially jointly satisfiable, and MAX-SAT retains nearly all of them regardless of source answer. Removing the 8.2\% non-discriminative constraints yields zero accuracy improvement. The discriminative majority does not provide independent signal; it reinforces the majority answer that SC would already select. This confirmation bias, not a lack of discrimination, explains why SICA degenerates to majority voting (Appendix~\ref{app:ablation}).
467	
468	
469	\section{Hyperparameter Sensitivity}
470	\label{app:sensitivity}
471	\label{sec:k-sensitivity}
472	\label{sec:t-sensitivity}
473

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section tests the three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nThe trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \\emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\\% of constraints on average ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| / |\\mathcal{C}|$, $p = 0.636$ between correct/incorrect), confirming degeneration (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Counterintuitively, jointly-wrong problems produce \\emph{more} constraints (41.9 vs.\\ 37.1 for correct): higher symbolic density amplifies rather than corrects bias.\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% zero cross-answer variable overlap), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). A premise-only experiment isolates the bias source: filtering out the 23\\% of constraints that reference the conclusion halves the accuracy gain ($+2.45$\\,pp, $p = 0.359$, n.s.), indicating that conclusion-referencing constraints account for approximately half of the constraint error. Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The bias ratio gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\\times$) at high SC produces the worst degradation ($-4.41$\\,pp). Error analysis reveals 64\\% (52/81) of joint SICA+SC errors predict \\textsc{Unknown} for ground-truth \\textsc{True}/\\textsc{False} problems---a systematic ``hedge to Unknown'' avoidance strategy---and 30\\% (24/81) are \\emph{confident systematic errors} where all 12 traces agree on the wrong answer. \\textsc{False} problems are disproportionately hard: 43\\% of joint errors vs.\\ 21\\% of correct predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-categorization}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\\sim}$88\\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Thirteen further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, premise-only filtering, DPP trace selection, and alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.",

    "newString": "confirm all four predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias and cannot break the same-model ceiling. The oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the aggregation mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution---escaping the mirage entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\\%) and 55.00\\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\\% ($\\Delta = +1.96$\\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.125$, n.s.; $b{=}0$, $c{=}4$); $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\\% ($+2.94$\\,pp, $p = 0.210$, n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}1$ yields $+0.83$\\,pp (n.s.), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\\,pp ($p = 0.013$), and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$). Notably, same-model SICA \\emph{degrades} ProofWriter performance ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), while the cross-architecture combo yields significant improvement---illustrating the Symbolic Mirage prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}) that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution. We report $w{=}3$ on ProofWriter as the primary finding; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction for six comparisons ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ (adjusted $p < 0.001$); all four FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. A per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \\textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\\% of predictions) and performs poorly on \\textsc{Unknown} (3.5\\% accuracy), mirroring the FOLIO pattern where NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.\n\n\\paragraph{Why it works.}\nThe 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, so they err on different subsets---satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). On FOLIO, this produces per-label complementarity precisely where the LLM is weakest: \\textsc{False} problems constitute 43\\% of joint errors (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), and NLI excels exactly there (71.43\\% vs.\\ SC's 38.10\\%) via contradiction detection, while SC dominates \\textsc{Unknown} (68.12\\% vs.\\ 18.84\\%).\n",

The oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the aggregation mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution---escaping the mirage entirely.",

    "oldString": "\\textsc{False} problems are disproportionately hard: 43\\% of joint errors vs.\\ 21\\% of correct predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-categorization}).     "newString": "\\textsc{False} problems are disproportionately hard: 43\\% of joint errors vs.\\ 21\\% of correct predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-categorization}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\\sim}$88\\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality. Bias intensifies along reasoning steps: late-step constraints (step $\\geq 7$) exhibit 27.2\\% conclusion leakage vs.\\ 22.0\\% for early steps (1--3; Fisher $p = 3 \\times 10^{-6}$). SICA restricted to late-only constraints degrades to 44.1\\% ($-10.3$\\,pp), while early-only reaches 56.4\\% ($+2.0$\\,pp), and cross-trace consistency decays from 24.5\\% (early) to 9.5\\% (late)---confirming prediction~(4) of \\S\\ref{sec:mirage}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section tests the four predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nThe trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \\emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\\% of constraints on average ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| / |\\mathcal{C}|$, $p = 0.636$ between correct/incorrect), confirming degeneration (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Counterintuitively, jointly-wrong problems produce \\emph{more} constraints (41.9 vs.\\ 37.1 for correct): higher symbolic density amplifies rather than corrects bias.\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% zero cross-answer variable overlap), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). A premise-only experiment isolates the bias source: filtering out the 23\\% of constraints that reference the conclusion halves the accuracy gain ($+2.45$\\,pp, $p = 0.359$, n.s.), indicating that conclusion-referencing constraints account for approximately half of the constraint error. Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The bias ratio gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\\times$) at high SC produces the worst degradation ($-4.41$\\,pp). Error analysis reveals 64\\% (52/81) of joint SICA+SC errors predict \\textsc{Unknown} for ground-truth \\textsc{True}/\\textsc{False} problems---a systematic ``hedge to Unknown'' avoidance strategy---and 30\\% (24/81) are \\emph{confident systematic errors} where all 12 traces agree on the wrong answer. \\textsc{False} problems are disproportionately hard: 43\\% of joint errors vs.\\ 21\\% of correct predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-categorization}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\\sim}$88\\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Thirteen further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, premise-only filtering, DPP trace selection, and alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017tSB3jor2XNXyqrVYJ2V6w`</summary>

```text
77	We test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.
78	
79	\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\Delta = -1.1$\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\pm 1$\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$) yields $\Delta = 0.00$\,pp.
80	
81	\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\Delta \approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\Delta = -9.31$\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\ref{app:remediation}).
82	
83	\paragraph{Additional directions.} Thirteen further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, premise-only filtering, DPP trace selection, and alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).
84	
85	\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp. All families cluster above $\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section tests the four predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nThe trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \\emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\\% of constraints on average ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| / |\\mathcal{C}|$, $p = 0.636$ between correct/incorrect), confirming degeneration (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Counterintuitively, jointly-wrong problems produce \\emph{more} constraints (41.9 vs.\\ 37.1 for correct): higher symbolic density amplifies rather than corrects bias.\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% zero cross-answer variable overlap), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). A premise-only experiment isolates the bias source: filtering out the 23\\% of constraints that reference the conclusion halves the accuracy gain ($+2.45$\\,pp, $p = 0.359$, n.s.), indicating that conclusion-referencing constraints account for approximately half of the constraint error. Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The bias ratio gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\\times$ weighted) at intermediate SC produces SICA's only positive trends; low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\\times$) at high SC produces the worst degradation ($-4.41$\\,pp). Error analysis reveals 64\\% (52/81) of joint SICA+SC errors predict \\textsc{Unknown} for ground-truth \\textsc{True}/\\textsc{False} problems---a systematic ``hedge to Unknown'' avoidance strategy---and 30\\% (24/81) are \\emph{confident systematic errors} where all 12 traces agree on the wrong answer. \\textsc{False} problems are disproportionately hard: 43\\% of joint errors vs.\\ 21\\% of correct predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-categorization}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that ${\\sim}$88\\% of the same-model potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality. Bias intensifies along reasoning steps: late-step constraints (step $\\geq 7$) exhibit 27.2\\% conclusion leakage vs.\\ 22.0\\% for early steps (1--3; Fisher $p = 3 \\times 10^{-6}$). SICA restricted to late-only constraints degrades to 44.1\\% ($-10.3$\\,pp), while early-only reaches 56.4\\% ($+2.0$\\,pp), and cross-trace consistency decays from 24.5\\% (early) to 9.5\\% (late)---confirming prediction~(4) of \\S\\ref{sec:mirage}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Thirteen further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, premise-only filtering, DPP trace selection, and alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.",
